Safety & Laser
Concepts
The safety guidelines provided in this lesson are intended to educate those who attend the technical training on all safety issues
in order to install, program, operate, and maintain the printer in a safe manner.
Lesson Objectives
l
Determine what are suitable safety glasses for working with Fiber lasers
l
Define fire hazards associated with VJ Fiber lasers
l
Define the electrical hazards associated with VJ Fiber lasers
l
Explain the dangers of fume hazards and how they can be prevented
l
Identify how to properly install beam shields, guards and interlocks
l
Identify general safety instructions and procedures
l
Define the different laser classifications
Lesson Activities
In this section, you will review the safety conventions to be used throughout the training. You will monitor one another’s adher -
ence to the safety guidelines and note when not in compliance. Students not in compliance with the safety guidelines will be
expected to comply upon notification.
What is a Laser?
A laser is "a device that produces radiant energy predominantly by stimulated emission. Laser radiation may be highly coherent
temporally, or spatially, or both" (ANSI Z136.1 - 2014).
The term "Laser" was originally coined as an acronym for L ight A mplified by S timulated E mission of R adiation.
9
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
Characteristics of a Laser Beam
l
Monochromatic
l
Light is of a single color or wave length
l
CO2 laser light is invisible to the human eye
l
Collimated
l
Laser light is "parallel" and has little divergence or spreading
l
Coherent
l
All light energy particles or photons travel in phase with each other
F IGURE 1- 1: An example of the monochromatic wavelength of laser light.
Product Marking Techniques
Unlike an ink or pigment based coders that apply a new material onto a substrate, lasers mark on products by altering the sub -
strate itself.
Ablation
Ablation is the removal of a layer to reveal contrasting layer below. The topmost layer of film or paint on the substrate is vapor -
ized.
10
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 2: A surface coating ablated off of a substrate.
Thermal or Chemical Change
In a thermal/chemical change technique, light and heat from the beam interact with the substrate to alter it at the surface
without changing its texture or removing substrate. On metals this is occurs as a function of surface oxides forming on the sub -
strate surface at the point of beam contact. Similarly on plastics, the beam spurs molecules of the substrate to react and change
color. If the plastic does not react, dyes or additives may be added to the plastic medium before formation. The beam will react
with those additives during marking.
11
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
F IGURE 1- 3: Substrates altered by thermal/chemical reactions at the surface.
Physical Change - Surface Modification
Lasers may mark on substrates by burning away substrate to a target depth (in the tens of microns). A trench is left behind in the
shape of the marked characters or graphics. Substrates that are too thin for engraving (e.g. plastic bottles) may simply be mod -
ified at the surface causing the formation of bubbles that raise up rather than leave behind a trench. Glass does not burn and is
marked by crazing , or shattering, at the point of beam contact.
Marks in Glass Substrates Sharpen Over Time
Since marks on glass substrates rely on crazing and not vaporization, the removed substrate does not always
immediately fall away. This means that at first a mark on the glass substrate may appear faint but will
sharpen as the substrate jostles on a conveyor and the tiny shards fall away.
Engraving
When engraving, a superficial incision removes a few microns of material. This occurs via melting/vaporization (metal), partial
burning (thermoplastic), or crazing (glass).
12
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 4: Surfaces marked via engraving.
Substrate Modification
When marking by substrate modification, the beam causes an additive effect on the substrate rather than a reductive one as
occurs in engraving. The surface is momentarily softened and then resolidifies. While softened, impurities such as oxides or
gasses are released that alter the color or texture of the substrate.
13
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
F IGURE 1- 5: Substrates marked via surface modification.
How Does a Laser Work?
Laser beams are the product of energy discharged from atoms as photons. This energy emits as electrons orbiting the nucleus of
the atoms decay to a lower energy level. The energy that formerly kept that electron moving at a higher orbit converts to a
photon.
Emissions may be spontaneous or stimulated . Spontaneous emissions occur naturally based on the interaction of excited
atoms in the electromagnetic field in their environment but are incoherent. Stimulated emissions occur when an incoming
photon of a specific frequency interacts with an excited atomic electron causing it to decay to a lower energy level. This inter -
action is called absorption .
Ordinarily absorption produces no photons as the electrons simply raise to a higher energy level. Lasers work because the envir -
onment inside the beams source is such that instead of raising to a higher energy level, electrons instead decay and the dif -
ference in energy is released as a second photon of the same phase, frequency, polarization, and direction of travel.
14
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 6: Illustration of Spontaneous Emission. The F IGURE 1- 7: Illustration of Absorption. An incoming
orbit of the electron decays and a photon photon is absorbed and the increase in
escapes. energy results in an electron rising to a
higher orbit.
F IGURE 1- 8: Illustration of the interaction of light and matter that occurs in stimulated emission. A single photon strikes a higher
orbit electron which drops to a lower orbit. The original photon and a second photon are emitted.
Stimulated Emission & Light Amplification
Energy is applied to the laser beam source to stimulate emission.
As photons move through the gain medium an avalanche effect occurs as collisions with electrons produce more and more
photons, all cohering and moving on together in same direction at the same wavelength.
15
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
F IGURE 1- 9: An illustration of the amplification or "avalanche" effect on photons in a gain medium.
16
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
Laser Wavelengths
Laser beams, being light and therefore a form of electromagnetic radiation, can be produced at varying wavelengths on the
EM spectrum .
Wavelength is the distance between one peak or crest of a light wave and the next peak or crest. It is equal
to the speed of the wave divided by its frequency, and to the speed of a wave times its period.
Electromagnetic radiation has varying effects on people and things. The radio waves, light waves, and X Rays are all
EM radiation. Radio Waves are harmless to people, light is dangerous only at some exposures and wavelengths, and X Rays are
dangerous at more than minimal occasional exposure.
F IGURE 1- 10: Where laser radiation wavelengths fall on the electromagnetic spectrum.
The primary wavelengths of laser radiation for current commercial applications fall within the Ultraviolet (UV), visible, and
infrared ranges of the spectrum. UV laser radiation wavelengths range between 180 and 400 nm. Visible light wavelengths range
between 400 and 700 nm. The infrared range of the EM spectrum consists of radiation at wavelengths between 700 nm and 1
mm.
17
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
Effects of Laser Radiation on Biological Tissue
Laser radiation absorbed by the skin penetrates only a few layers. In the eye, visible and near infrared radiation passes through
the cornea and is focused on and absorbed by the retina. It is the wavelength of the light that determines the visible sensation of
color: violet at 400 nm, red at 700 nm, and the other colors of the visible spectrum in between. When radiation is absorbed by bio -
logical tissue, the effect on that tissue is either photochemical , thermal , or mechanical .
l
In the ultraviolet range the effect is primarily photochemical.
l
In the infrared range the effect is primarily thermal.
l
In the visible range both effects are present.
l
When the intensity of the radiation is sufficiently high, the result will be damage to the absorbing tissue.
Fiber laser beams have a wavelength of 1064 nm and therefore falls into the visible to near infrared spectrum (i.e., 400 - 1400
nm). Laser light in the visible to near infrared spectrum can cause retinal damage resulting in scotoma (blind spot in the fovea).
This wave band is therefore also know as the retinal hazard region .

l
High radiation intensity causes a local heating and burning of the cornea and thus a reduction or loss of sight!
Take appropriate measures to protect people working near the laser against beam exposure!
All personnel within the room must wear safety goggles suitable against laser radiation while performing main -
tenance or adjustments at the open laser housing or near an open beam delivery system!
Never look directly into the laser beam!
The skin can withstand higher radiation intensities than the eye. However, depending on the radiation exposure period and radi -
ation intensity, the radiation burns and destroys tissue.
Protect your skin by wearing adequate protective clothing! Avoid any exposure of your skin to laser radiation!
Do not allow the laser beam to contact your clothing!

The Complete Laser System
A complete and closed laser system acts as a class 1 laser system in normal operation. Laser radiation emitted by the laser source
is held completely within shielding until it reaches the product. The shielding prevents emergence of laser radiation or laser beam
19

reflection.
Care of System Umbilical
The minimum bending radius of the umbilical cable is 85 mm. The umbilical is made for stationary use. It will
also sustain slow moving with low acceleration a few times a day.
System Overheat
Pay close attention to the laser's air flow. Failure to do so will result in damage to the laser source or over -
heating of the system boards.
l
The laser system is cooled by air. The internal cooling system is designed to supply sufficient cooling.
l
Make sure that the cooling air can be drawn in and blown out freely and that there is sufficient air exchange
at the installation site to ensure heat dissipation.
Radiation Exposure Hazard - Eye
F IGURE 1- 11: Illustrated visual impact of eye damage caused by laser radiation.
To understand the possible health effects, it is important to understand the functions of the major parts of the human eye.
The Human Eye
The cornea is the transparent layer of tissue covering the eye. Damage to the outer cornea may be uncomfortable (like a gritty
feeling) or painful, but will usually heal quickly. Damage to deeper layers of the cornea may cause permanent injury.
20
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 12: Parts of the human eye.
The cornea is a clear, dome- shaped surface that covers the front of the eye. It is the first and most powerful lens in the eye's
optical system. To keep it transparent the cornea contains no blood vessels. Tears that flow over it and aqueous humor in the
chamber behind it keep it nourished. When you hear of eye transplants, it is the cornea that is being replaced. The cornea can be
damaged by accidents, infections, and genetic defects.
The pupil is the black circle in the center of the eye, and its primary function is to monitor the amount of light that comes into
the eye. When there is a lot of light, the pupil contracts to keep the light from overwhelming the eye. When there is very little
light, the pupil expands so it can soak up as much as possible.
The iris is the colored part of the eye. Although it might seem purely cosmetic, the iris actually functions to adjust the size of the
pupil. It has muscles that contract or expand depending on the amount of light the pupil needs to process images.
The lens exists behind the pupil and is responsible for allowing your eyes to focus on small details like words in a book. The lens is
in a constant state of adjustment as it becomes thinner or thicker to accommodate the detailed input it receives. With age, the
lens loses a lot of its elasticity which often results in cataracts and presbyopia, i.e. nearsightedness, because the lens cannot
adjust as well to its surroundings as it used to.
The vitreous humour is a gel- like substance that helps to keep the eyeball in its proper, circular shape. This is the area in the eye
where floaters develop as pieces of the vitreous humor clump together and cast shadows onto the retina. With age, the vitreous
humor begins to shrink and can cause problems like posterior retinal detachment or retinal tears.
The retina is the area at the back of the eye that receives the refined, visual message from the front of the eye, and it transmits
that visual message to the brain using electrical signals.
The sclera is the white part of the eye, and its main function is to provide strength, structure, and protection for the eye. The
sclera contains blood vessels that can tell an eye doctor a lot about the overall state of your health.
Ocular Absorption by Wavelength
Damage to the eye is dependent upon the wavelength of the beam.
21
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
F IGURE 1- 13: Ocular absorption by wavelength, illustrated.
l
Ultraviolet and low energy far- infrared radiation can injure the epithelial layer of the cornea; a condition that is painful and
visually handicapping. At lower powers, this injury is primarily due to a photochemical reaction. A latency period of hours
may exist between the time of exposure and the development of the corneal pathology. Minimal corneal lesions heal within
a few days, but meanwhile hinder vision.
l
High energy far- infrared radiation is mainly absorbed by the cornea producing immediate burns at all corneal layers. An
infrared laser can produce a burn resulting in immediate visual incapacitation and may lead to permanent corneal scarring.
Very high energy radiation can perforate the cornea which may result in complete loss of the eye.
l
Laser light in the visible to near infrared spectrum (i.e., 400 - 1400 nm) can cause damage to the retina resulting in scotoma
(blind spot in the fovea). This wave band is also know as the "retinal hazard region". Exposure to the Q- switched Nd:YAG
laser beam (1064 nm) is especially hazardous and may initially go undetected because the beam is invisible and the retina
lacks pain sensory nerves. Photoacoustic retinal damage may be associated with an audible "pop" at the time of exposure.
Visual disorientation due to retinal damage may not be apparent to the operator until considerable thermal damage has
occurred.
22
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 14: Example of retinal damage.
Figure 1- 14 above depicts a human eye injury resulting from four pulses into the macular region from an AN/GVS- 5 Nd: YAG
laser rangefinder. The pulse duration was about 20 ns and the pulse energy was about 15 mJ. The safe exposure limit for this
pulse duration is 2 mJ per pulse. Thus, this exposure was 7500 times the safe level.
Short pulse lasers produce the greatest eye hazards as each short pulse results in a tiny explosion in the retina. The resulting shock
wave causes severe damage to the retinal tissue.
The photo in Figure 1- 14 above was taken three weeks after the exposure. It shows the permanent destruction of the macular
region. Visual acuity in the eye is approximately 20/400 and will not improve.
This injury could not have occurred if the individual had been wearing the appropriate laser safety eyewear .
F IGURE 1- 15: Short pulse retinal damage, absorption of 1064nm wavelength.
Figure 1- 15 above depicts the eye of a rhesus monkey which received a single short pulse into the eye. The laser energy was
absorbed in a small volume of the retina. Most of the energy was absorbed in a thin layer at the back of the retina called the pig -
ment epithelium. This is a layer pigmented by melanin, the substance that colors skin. This layer absorbs most of the light enter -
ing the eye, preventing light scattering in the eye that would blur vision. When a short laser pulse is absorbed into this layer, the
energy heats the melanin grains, the thermal energy is transferred to the surrounding water, and the result is a microscopic
steam explosion. The shock wave from this explosion ruptures blood vessels in the surrounding tissue, causing hemorrhaging in
the ocular medium and inside the retinal layers. This can be seen as the darkened area surrounding the lesion. This part of the ret -
ina will die. The resulting blind spot will destroy the central vision of this eye.
23
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
F IGURE 1- 16: A multiple pulse retinal injury. Image color enhanced to better show the laser damage.
Figure 1- 16 above depicts the retina of a human who experienced an eye injury from a repetitive pulse near infrared laser. The
beam was invisible. In such cases people do not usually realize they are exposed until their vision has been severely affected. This
person’s eye was moving during this exposure, resulting in a line of laser burns across the retina.
The macula of the eye is located out of the photo to the lower left. This individual was fortunate that the damage did not extend
into the macula.
Radiation Exposure Hazard - Skin
Skin is the largest organ of the body and, as such, is at the greatest risk for coming in contact with the laser beam. The head,
hands, and arms are the most likely skin surfaces to be exposed.
24
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
l
Stratum Corneum (Dead Layer)
The stratum corneum is the outermost layer. It consists of flattened, dead epidermal cells that protect the living tissue from
water loss, injury from physical objects, and radiant energy. The thickness is 8- 20 μm except on the soles of the feet and the
palms of the hands where it is 500- 600 μm.
l
Epidermis
The epidermis is the outermost layer of living tissue, where the tanning process takes place. It has a relatively uniform thick -
ness of about 50- 150 μm.
l
Dermis Corium
The dermis corium is made up largely of connective tissue which gives the skin its elasticity and supportive strength.
Included in this layer are nerve cells, blood vessels, and lymphatic glands. The thickness of this layer varies over the body
from 1- 4 mm.
l
Subcutaneous Tissue
The subcutaneous tissue is made up mostly of fatty tissue serving as insulation and as a shock absorption medium. The
thickness of this layer varies according to the area of the body as well as from person to person.
Dermal Damage by Radiation Varies by Wavelength
Laser effects on tissue depend on the power density of the incident beam, absorption of tissues at the incident wavelength, time
beam is held on tissue, and the effects of blood circulation and heat conduction in the affected area.
25
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
F IGURE 1- 17: Effects of laser radiation by wavelength.
Immediate Effects of Exposure
The immediate effect of exposure to laser light above the biological damage threshold is normally tissue burns. Injury to the skin
can be caused by either temperature elevation in skin tissues or from a photochemical effect (e.g., "sunburn") from excessive
levels of actinic ultraviolet radiation.
26
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 18: Examples of skin burns.
Delayed Effects of Exposure
The possibility of adverse effects from repeated or chronic laser irradiation to the skin has been suggested, although it is normally
discounted. Only optical radiation in the ultraviolet region of the spectrum has been shown to cause long- term, delayed effects
such as accelerated skin aging and skin cancer. At present, laser safety standards for exposure of the skin attempt to take these
adverse effects into account.
Burns, skin cancer and acceleration of skin aging are possible from exposure to laser radiation. Chronic exposure can cause
increased pigmentation.
Fire Hazards
Understand fire hazards, how to prevent them, and how to react to fires.
Elements of a Fire
l
Oxygen
l
Heat
l
Fuel
Fire Hazard Terminology
l
Flash Point
Minimum temperature at which a liquid will burn. The Flashpoint for Methyl Ethyl Keytone (MEK) 73°F.
27
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
l
Flammable Range
Environmental conditions in which there is a fuel/air mixture that leads to combustion, e.g not too lean (too little fuel), not
too rich (too much fuel).
l
Lower Flammability Limit (LFL)
The lower limit of the flammable range. Also know as Lower Explosive Limit (LEL).
l
Upper Flammability Limit (UFL)
The upper limit of the flammable range. Also known as Upper Explosive Limit (UEL).
l
Bonding
Connecting two objects or pieces of equipment with a conductive cable to maintain the same electrical potential between
the two. Grounding
Classes of Fire
Fires are classified as one of four types.
Class A - Ordinary Combustibles
Most common fire. A burning house is a class A fire.
Class B - Flammable Liquids
Liquid combustibles such as paint strippers, solvents, fuels, oil, or grease.
Class C - Electrical Equipment
Additional danger from electrical shock. Power equipment OFF immediately.
Class D - Combustible Metals
Combustible metals such as magnesium, aluminum, and metallic sodium. Metals burn easier in powder or chip form.
DO NOT apply water to a Class D fire as this can result in an explosion.
Fire Prevention & Safety
l
Always store flammable liquids in approved cabinets.
l
Never operate the laser system on unattended material.
l
Always keep the area around the machine clean and free of clutter, combustible materials, explosives, or volatile solvents
such as acetone, alcohol, or gasoline.
l
Always keep a properly maintained and inspected fire extinguisher on hand. A Halotron fire extinguisher or a multi- purpose
dry- chemical fire extinguisher. The Halotron extinguisher emits a clean, easily removable substance that is not harmful to
the mechanics or wiring of the laser system. Other dry chemical extinguisher can sometimes emit a sticky, corrosive powder
that is very difficult to clean up.
l
Always use a fume extractor when vector printing.
l
Many materials have the potential to burst suddenly into flames – even materials with which you might be very familiar.
Utilizing the Air Assist feature will reduce the instance of flare- ups when working with flammable materials such as acrylic
and wood.
l
Keep your laser system clean - Build up of cutting and engraving residue and debris is dangerous and can create a fire
hazard in its own right. These small pieces present a very dangerous fire hazard, especially if they are allowed to collect over
time. Since most users cut wood and acrylic, these small pieces that fall into the conveyor, extractor nozzle or build up
around the lens act just like kindling and can ignite and start a fire.
28
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
l
Lastly, make sure the exhaust blower you are using receives proper maintenance. Periodically clean the exhaust blower and
duct system to remove built- up debris. If you detect odor while engraving, or smoke in the cabinet is visible in the area of
the lens carriage, inspect the exhaust system for leaks and obstructions. Inspect and clean the exhaust ports in your
machine to ensure there are no obstructions within the machine itself. Use a wire brush to clean the plenum and exhaust
port of your machine.

Laser Classification
A laser's class is an indication of the beam hazard level of that laser or laser system during normal operation or the determination
thereof. The hazard level of a laser or laser system is represented by a number or a numbered capital letter. The laser clas -
sifications are Class 1, Class 1M, Class 2, Class 2M, Class 3R, Class 3B, and Class 4. (ANSI Z136.1- 2014)
Laser Class 1
l
No danger to the eyes (also with the use of optical instruments).
l
No danger to the skin.
l
The accessible laser radiation is, under reasonably foreseeable conditions, harmless.
The products are absolutely laser- light safe (eye & skin): no laser light at critical power levels can leave the laser system. This is
the standard for laser marking machines. Laser Class 1 products are operated in the same manner as conventional machine tools.
Example 1: Marking on Metal Strips
Metal strips are led through a marking cabin closed in on all sides with steel plates (green arrow in Figure 1- 21 below ). The laser
heads are located outside of the marking cabin. The writing heads are sealed all the way around by brushes (red arrows in Figure
1- 21 below ). The product intake and discharge areas are sealed with rows of brushes (red arrow).

Laser Class 2
l
No danger to the eyes at short exposure times (max. 0.25 s).
l
No danger to the skin.
l
The accessible laser radiation is only in the visible range (400 to 700 nm).
Short irradiation (up to 0.25 s) is not dangerous for the eye. The eye protects itself via the eyelid- closing reflex. For CW lasers of
this class the maximum accessible power level is 1 mW.
Caution
Class 2 lasers can be operated without any safety covers or housings if it is guaranteed that the view into the
laser beam cannot last longer than 0.25 s.
Laser Class 2M
l
No danger to the eyes at short exposure times (max. 0.25 s). As long as no optical instruments are used!
l
No danger to the skin.
Same as Class 2, but safe for short time viewing only for the naked eye. Eye injury may occur when exposure occurs while using
optical instruments (e.g. binoculars, magnification glasses). As long as no optical focusing components are used the risk assess -
ment is not higher than in laser class 1M. Laser power less than 1mW
Caution
When optical components are used the risk assessment can be comparable with laser class 3R or 3B.
Laser Class 3R
l
Low risk to eyes
l
No risk to skin
The accessible laser radiation becomes dangerous for the eye when the beam diameter is reduced via optical components/ instru -
ments. If this is not the case and the radiation visible (400 to 700 nm) short irradiation (up to 0.25 s) is not dangerous for the
eye. In all other cases (diameter reduction, non- visible radiation, long irradiation) the eye is endangered.
The risk of eye damage is reduced because the maximum beam energy is limited to 5 times of the maximum energy of class 2.
Laser power less than 500mW.
Laser Class 3B
l
Medium to high risk to eyes
l
Low risk to skin
The accessible laser radiation is dangerous for the eye and in specific cases also for the skin.

Laser Class 4
l
Hazardous to eyes
l
Hazardous to skin
High power lasers (cw: 500 mW or greater, pulsed: 10 J/cm2 or the diffuse reflection limit) are hazardous to view under any con -
dition (directly or diffusely scattered) and are a potential fire hazard, LGAC hazard, and a skin hazard. Significant controls are
required of Class 4 laser facilities.
Interaction of Light & Matter
Most absorbing materials satisfy the Beer- Lambert Law.
An isolated atom scatters light because the electric field of the incident light wave forces the electrons in the atom to oscillate
back and forth about their equilibrium position. By the laws of electromagnetism, when a charge changes its velocity, it emits
radiation.
40
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
Light is emitted uniformly in all directions in the plane ∞ to oscillation, but decreases in amplitude as the viewing angle shifts
away from that plane.
l
Reflection occurs when light is scattered in the opposite direction of incident light.
l
Refraction is a phenomenon in which light is scattered in the forward direction and combines with the incident beam. The
physical effect of this combination is to make the transmitted light appear as though it has traveled more slowly through
the sample than through a vacuum.
l
Incident ray in physics, a ray of light which hits a surface.
l
The refracted ray or transmitted ray corresponding to a given incident ray represents the light that is transmitted
through the surface. The angle between this ray and the normal is known as the angle of refraction, and it is given by Snell's
Law. Conservation of energy requires that the power in the incident ray must equal the sum of the power in the transmitted
ray, the power in the reflected ray, and any power absorbed at the surface.
Reflection of Light
In the case of free propagation of a laser beam, special attention should be placed as the vision of the radiation is directly reflec -
ted, because of exposure to the eye may be exposed. Less dangerous is the vision of widespread radiation.
Intrabeam exposure means that the eye or skin is exposed directly to all or part of the laser beam. The eye or skin is exposed to
the full irradiance or radiant exposure possible.
Reflection is the return of radiant energy (incident light) by a surface, with no change in wavelength.
Diffuse Reflection takes place when different parts of a beam incident on a surface are reflected over a wide range of angles in
accordance with Lambert's Law. The intensity will fall off as the inverse of the square of the distance away from the surface and
also obey a Cosine Law of reflection.
Note
Direct (intrabeam) veiwing is the most dangerous of the three types.
41
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
Specular vs Diffuse Reflection
F IGURE 1- 27: The difference between light reflected on different surfaces.
Reflection off of smooth surfaces such as mirrors or a calm body of water leads to a type of reflection known as specular reflec -
tion . Reflection off of rough surfaces such as clothing, paper, and the asphalt roadway leads to a type of reflection known as dif -
fuse reflection . Whether the surface is microscopically rough or smooth has a tremendous impact upon the subsequent
reflection of a beam of light. The diagram below depicts two beams of light incident upon a rough and a smooth surface.
A light beam can be thought of as a bundle of individual light rays which are traveling parallel to each other. Each individual light
ray of the bundle follows the law of reflection. If the bundle of light rays is incident upon a smooth surface, then the light rays
reflect and remain concentrated in a bundle upon leaving the surface. On the other hand, if the surface is microscopically rough,
the light rays will reflect and diffuse in many different directions.
Specular reflections are mirror- like reflections and can reflect close to 100% of the incident light. Flat surfaces will not change a
fixed beam diameter, only the direction. Convex surfaces will cause beam spreading, and concave surfaces will make the beam
converge.
Diffuse reflections result when surface irregularities scatter light in all directions. The specular nature of a surface is dependent
upon the wavelength of incident radiation. A specular surface is one that has a surface roughness less than the wavelength of
the incident light. A very rough surface is not specular to visible light but might be to IR radiation of 10.6 µm from a CO2 laser.
Why a rough Surface Diffuses a Light Beam
For each type of reflection, each individual ray follows the law of reflection. However, the roughness of the material means that
each individual ray meets a surface which has a different orientation. The normal line at the point of incidence is different for dif -
ferent rays. Subsequently, when the individual rays reflect off the rough surface according to the law of reflection, they scatter in
different directions. The result is that the rays of light are incident upon the surface in a concentrated bundle and are diffused
upon reflection.
Counter Measures
l
Make sure that the working distance is set correctly before marking any reflecting material. Use a weakly reflecting material
for testing, e.g. laminated paper.
l
When marking reflecting material try not to mark in the center of the marking field. Position the product so that the mark -
ing is in the edge of the marking field.
l
When setting the laser power start with the maximum value and reduce the power until the marking result is satisfying. If
no marking is visible at maximum power the material cannot be marked using this laser.
42

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
General Laser Safety
The laser system must only be used for the treatment of material surfaces. The surfaces are locally heated by intensive class 4
laser radiation and are modified thereby. These systems are mainly applied for marking of product surfaces (expiry dates, batch
printing, serial numbers, etc.).
The radiation produced by the laser system is of high energy and therefore improper use represents a danger to persons or
objects!
Never expose human beings or animals to laser radiation.
This might result in severe burns of eyes or skin.
Never expose flammable materials to laser radiation!
Ensure the laser beam is housed in a shielded enclosure. Paper (circuit diagrams, leaflets, posters on the walls,
etc.), curtains not treated with flame retardant, wooden boards, or similar flammable materials can easily
ignite by direct or reflected laser radiation. Install smoke detectors, fire detectors, or similar or similar safety
equipment to detect fire should it occur.
Never expose reflective surfaces to laser radiation!
Never expose reflective surfaces to laser radiation! The reflected laser beam may cause the same dangers - in
some cases even greater dangers - as the original laser beam.
Never expose unknown materials to laser radiation!
Some materials (e.g. polyethylene, polypropylene, glass) are easily penetrated by the laser beam, although
they seem to be opaque for the human eye.
Danger of explosion! Make sure that the laser area is free of flam -
mable/explosive materials and vapors!.
Arbitrary modifications or changes to the laser system are forbidden.
If a modification made by the user to a previously classified laser system leads to a change of its performance
data and/or its intended use that person or organization performing the modification is responsible for a new
classification and labeling of the laser system. The person or organization is then regarded as the man -
ufacturer .
The laser system must be operated with open laser and/or open
beam delivery system by specially trained personnel only!
Observe all laser protection rules.
Feedback Radiation
When marking highly reflective material there might be a feedback of radiation into the laser. In this case the
system switches to fatal error mode.
A reset has to be carried out to continue: the laser must be switched off and on again.

Fiber laser enclosure materials must be entirely opaque and able to withstand the expected energy density, meaning they will not
be made from plastics and will likely be constructed of aluminum or steel . While there are some translucent materials that are
acceptable in the enclosure (constructed of material in accordance with DIN EN 207) these are materials are costly and likely
would not comprise the entire cabinet.

Automatically Moving Products
If a moving product is stopped to be marked (marking at standstill) the marking area should be screened for every marking by an
automatically moving laser beam protection. The beam protection must cover the surrounding contours of the marking area com -
pletely so that the entire marking area is screened light- tight towards the outside. That means there must be no gaps. That
applies also to the side facing the opening from which the laser beam is emitted. In case of malfunction where the marking pro -
cess is started and there is no product in the marking area, laser radiation must not exit the beam protection at any side.
If the product to be marked is moving during the marking process, e.g. on a conveyer belt, the construction of a beam protection
tunnel is necessary. In order to control the transmission of laser radiation, the beam- emergence opening is encased together with
the conveyer belt from above and from the sides. If the radiation might penetrate the conveyer belt, e.g. at gaps, the tunnel
must also be installed from below. The tunnel must be long enough in front of and behind the marking area to prevent possible
reflections coming from the product itself, or other surfaces (e.g. metallic jointed- band conveyer), from exiting at the sides of the
46
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
tunnel. The tunnel must be long enough to absorb reflections before such reflections reach the ends of the tunnel. The tunnel
should be constructed to be as close as possible to the product. Thus potential exits for laser radiation are minimized. If possible
flaps, brushes or strip curtains should be installed at the ends of the tunnel to provide additional screening.

Is the OD for the worst- case situation?
Have different frame types been evaluated?

Optical Density of Protective Eyewear
When selecting protective eye wear for working near lasers, one must select glasses or goggles with Optical Density (OD) appro -
priate to the wavelength of the laser in use. OSHA defines OD as "a logarithmic expression for the attenuation produced by an
attenuating medium, such as an eye protection filter." Stated more mathematically, the ANSI Z136.1 document for the Safe Use
of Lasers defines OD as "The logarithm to the base ten of the reciprocal of the transmittance at a particular wavelength ."
How OD is Calculated
We will demonstrate the methodology behind calculating OD. However, if you are unsure what safety equipment to use consult
your Laser Safety Officer (LSO).
OD Level 6 Required
All Videojet Technologies, INC employees working with lasers shall wear glasses with an OD of 6 at the
wavelength in use.
There are several variable symbols used in the OD calculations and prerequisite calculations.
Value Symbol Calculated As
Optical Density Dƛ
Transmittance (Overexposure) τƛ
Exposure H
p
Maximum Permissible Exposure MPE Refer to ANSI Z136.1 - 2014 Table 5a
48
Rev AC

Safety & Laser Concepts
The MPE determination is based on the duration of use, and particular wavelength.
l
Aversion response to visible lasers (0.4 to 0.7 um, and class 3B and 4 lasers). The aversion response time of 0.25 s shall be
used when long term intrabeam viewing is not intended.
l
Near Infrared (0.7 to 1.4 um, and class 3B and 4 lasers). 10 s shall be used as a “worst case” time duration for MPE determ -
ination for this category of lasers.
l
Diffuse Viewing (intended class 3B and 4). Maximum viewing times may be used within an 8 hour time period based on the
operation, such as with alignment procedures where viewing is intended.
l
Diffuse Viewing (unintended class 3B and 4). 600 seconds shall be used for this category of laser classes where there is
potential for intense staring during an operation.
l
Daily Occupational Exposure (long term, class 3B and 4). 30,000 s shall be used for long term 8 hour exposure durations to
low level light where applicable.
OD Calculation Example
An example OD calculation for a direct 100 watt fiber laser beam. While no Videojet Technologies, INC fiber laser generally oper -
ates at this wattage, for safety purposes we use the highest possible wattage with an additional margin added to ensure all pos -
sible wattages are covered.
Optical Density = Log (203922) = 5.309
10
Round up to an OD of 6.
OD Level 6 Required
All Videojet Technologies, INC employees working with lasers shall wear glasses with an OD of 6 at the
wavelength in use.
Nominal Hazard Zone
Nominal Hazard Zone (NHZ) is a distance within which the irradiance of a beam is greater than the MPE. Besides being specific
to a given wavelength and time of exposure, a different NHZ can be defined for the beam’s path to your eye (direct viewing),
specular reflectance, and diffuse reflectance.
49
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
The NHZ is a practical definition; it has a specific shape around your particular laboratory apparatus. For instance, assuming your
lab has no windows and a solid door, the NHZ will in the worst case scenario be the area (e.g. square footage) of the lab itself. In
other words, the NHZ for you will be derived at the end of your safety calculations, and thereafter will be most useful to you for
planning control measures in you laboratory.
Nominal Hazard Zone Example Calculation
An example NHZ calculation for a 100 watt Fiber laser beam.

To solve an equation with an exponent such as :
1. Enter the base , in this example that is 1274 .
x y
2. Press y OR  x (depicted differently on different calculators).
1/2
3. Enter the exponent, in this example that is 1/2 or 0.5 . The simplified result of (1274) should
be 35.69 out to several decimal places.
4. Press x or * to multiply.
5. Enter 42.3 . Your result should be 1509.8 .

System Overview
Performance Level Rating
Performance Level ratings are derived from international machinery design standard ISO- 13849- 1. Applying ISO- 13849- 1
assesses the risk of injury to humans from the system versus the system's contribution to risk reduction. A lower performance rat -
ing means a system has less mitigation in place to prevent injury and a higher level (Performance Level e ) means that greater risk
mitigation is in place. Performance Level ratings for lasers are dependent both on the hardware included inside the laser (boards)
and the parts the system employs outside of the laser such as door switches and cables. The rating is affected by both what
these components are and how the system interacts with them. European users will typically be most concerned with per -
formance level ratings.
Additional Parts Needed for Performance Level
In all of the following the Performance Level Rating is only achieved the following are also present: positive
break interlock NC- contacts, single screened interlock wires (cross wire short detection), and push button
manual reset button are used. Any deviation from this results in no performance level. Otherwise no per -
formance level rating.
With the Standard SPM board only:
l
Shutterlock
No performance level rating is achieved.
l
Interlock
Performance level d is achieved, but only if mechanical positive break interlock NC- contacts, single screened interlock
wires (cross wire short detection), and a push button manual reset button are used. Any deviation from this results in no
performance level. Otherwise, no performance level rating is achieved.
With the Optional CPD and FASS:
l
Shutterlock/Door Circuit
Performance level d is achieved.
l
Interlock/Emergency Circuit
Performance level e is achieved. Push button manual reset button required.

Keypad & Keyswitch
The membrane keypad and keyswitch are located on the front panel of the supply unit.
57
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
Keypad LEDs
The keypad is a single membrane with integrated LEDs for the following functions:
l
Beam Shutter Open - Illuminates red when the shutter is open.
l
Emissions - Illuminates red when the laser source is powered and capable of marking.
l
Error - Flashes yellow when an Error occurs
l
System Indicator - Five green LEDs that show the progress during boot process and flash as mark is made during marking
process.
Keypad Buttons
There are also three buttons integrated into the membrane keypad:
l
Power On/Standby - If system is off, pressing this button it starts the boot process. If the unit is already on, pressing this
button switches the system to Standby Mode.
Note that power is always ON unless the main power switch on rear of unit is turned off.
l
Start Marking - Puts laser into marking mode and will mark either immediately or when a trigger is received.
l
Stop Marking - Stops the marking process.
58
Rev AC

System Overview
Keyswitch
The keyswitch is accessible from the front panel of the supply unit.
l
In the 1 position, the keyswitch allows power to the beam source. In this state, the laser is armed and capable of creating
a beam .
l
Remove the key from the keyswitch when servicing the laser to prevent unintended firing of the laser.

Supply Unit Rear
The supply unit of the 7340/7440 laser system houses all power supplies, the laser source, and main circuit boards for the system.
F IGURE 2- 3: Rear of supply unit with standard connectors.
The customer interface plate and communications plate all have options which may be ordered from the factory or upgraded in
the field. For details on these options, see "Customer Interface Connector Options" on the facing page and "Communications
Plate Options" on page 64 .
Power Switch: Controls the main power to the system. When in the On position, the unit will have voltage supplied to main con -
trol board and keypad, but will be in Standby mode until the front panel On button is pushed, starting the boot process. When the
power switch is in the off position, there is no voltage supplied to the unit internally.
Fuses: Two 8 Amp fuses are contained in the fuse insert. These are necessary for supplying system power. The insert is removed
by pressing in on both top and bottom tab at the same time.

F IGURE 2- 8: Quick Connector Plate, AL- 79739.

There is also an RS- 232 connector for serial communication and a small pass through. You can use the pass through for routing a
small cable through and connecting its wires on the Customer Interface connectors on Chameleon board.
This connector option comes with two jumper plugs: one for interlock and one for fume extractor. If the jumper plugs are not fit -
ted in place and no DIN connector from an interlock and fume extractor are connected, errors will occur. The fume extractor
jumper plug can also be used as a connector, but the interlock jumper plug is permanently sealed and can only function as a
jumper. A 7 pin DIN with flying leads is necessary to connect door switches or other safety components to the laser system.

F IGURE 2- 9: Cable passage plate, AL- 79740.
This connector plate has 10 cable passage blocks (2 each of 5 different sizes). To route cables through the blocks, unscrew the
top 2 screws of the frame and slide the block out through the top of the frame. The blocks are cut on one side to allow the white
filler to slide out and be replaced by the cable. You can then slide the block back into place. Blocks are held in position by pressure
when you retighten the screws on top of the frame.

F IGURE 2- 10: Standard plate with RJ45 Ethernet connector identified.

System Overview
Dual RJ45 Port Plate
In applications where two Ethernet connections are necessary on a single laser system, you can order the system with a dual
RJ45 port connector plate installed. This is necessary in applications that require multiple network ports, such as connecting the
laser both directly to a local computer and to a network device such as a network switch.
F IGURE 2- 11: The optional dual RJ45 Ethernet plate identified on the rear of the supply unit.
TU440/TU430 Connector
To connect the optional TU440/TU430 touchscreen monitor, a specialized HDMI port must be installed on the rear of the supply
unit. The TU440/TU430 touchscreen monitor can only be used to access the TCS+ user interface integrated into the operating
system of the laser. You can also access TCS+ via any device with web browser connected directly the laser's LAN Ethernet port or
otherwise on the same LAN as the laser.
Laser units connect to the TU440/TU430 through a specialized HDMI connector and cable which carry both signal and power
for the touchscreen.
Possible Equipment Damage - Powered HDMI Port
Never plug a standard monitor into the laser HDMI port as voltage at the HDMI port will damage the monitor.

System Overview
Marking Head Sensor and Holders
An optional sensor is available to create an error when the marking unit is removed from the holder. This sensor must be used
with one of the two marking head holders for the 7340/7440 and cannot be used with any other marking head holder. The
7340/7440 marking head holders use a metal internal ring that is sensed by the optional sensor. Other marking head holders,
including those for continuous ink jet, use a plastic ring which will not be detected by the optional sensor.
Marking Head Holders
Only the following marking head holders will function properly with the sensor. These holders have a special design with a metal
screw on the internal ring that attaches to marking head. The sensor only detects metal. Most ink jet marking head holders have
a plastic ring that surrounds the marking head and therefore the laser system will always display that the marking head is
removed since it isn't detecting the metal screw.
l
AL- SP79890 (EXTENSION KIT,HOLDER,DOVETAIL,CFX): Dovetail attachment - use to connect to flat dovetail- style brackets
l
AL- SP80425 (EXTENSION KIT,HOLDER,TRIPOD,TUBE,38MM,CFX): Tube attachment - use to connect to stands with 38 mm
tube

Wireless Client Adapter for Laser Units
A Wireless Client Adapter (WCA) kit is available specifically for use with lasers using TCS+ or Videojet Remote Service. The WCA
kit is only a WiFi adapter that allows a laser to join a LAN via WiFi. It does not create a WiFi network for you.
The laser WiFi kit part number is AL- 78968. There is a special kit (AL- SP79930) for the 7340/7440 laser models that includes the
kit plus an additional special cable.
AL- 78968 WiFi Kit
The kit includes a MikroTik WiFi adapter, two Ethernet cables, Power over Ethernet (PoE) adapter, and multiple AC power cords to
power the adapter. Instructions for setting up the WiFi adapter are provided with the kit.
68
Rev AC

System Overview
F IGURE 2- 17: Kit contents of AL- 78968.
Use AC power only
The WiFi adapter should never be powered using the USB port on the Chameleon board. Using the USB for
power can create issues for the laser and/or application as the USB port does not provide enough power for
the MikroTik.
The difference between WiFi kit for other Videojet products (Part number: 393916) and the laser WiFi kit (AL- 78968) is the inclu -
sion of the multiple AC power cords with the laser specific kit. If 393916 has the AC power cord to fit your region, you can install
that kit to your laser in place of AL- 78968.
AL- SP79930 WiFi Kit for 7340/7440 only
AL- SP79930 WiFi kit for 7340/7440 also includes a cable that allows the MikroTik client to use the 48V on terminal blocks 7 and
8 within the 7340/7440 model instead of connecting AC power cord. The kit of 7340/7440 includes all the items in the AL- 78968
WiFi kit for all lasers plus this additional cable that is only for 7340/7440.

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
Chameleon Multi- Core Industrial
Protocols Boards (MCIP)
The Chameleon MCIP board is the main controller board for the laser system and is located within the supply unit. Note that the
Chameleon board must be at CMark v4.7 or higher to recognize the 7340/7440 laser.
The MCIP version of Chameleon board uses a multi- core Q7 processor module and has a connector for Miscellaneous Interface
Controller (MIC) board. The connector for the MIC board and color of the board differentiate the Chameleon MCIP from pre -
vious versions of Chameleon used in Videojet lasers.
The minimum operating system version required for the Chameleon multi- core boards (green, black and blue) is CMark version
4.1 if not using a MIC board and CMark version 4.5 or newer if using the optional MIC board for EtherNet/IP™ or PROFINET.
Chameleon MCIP2 Artix (red board) requires minimum CMark version 4.15.1 to function and minimum rescue stick image of
PlexMark v 1.14 for recovery.
7340/7440 Minimum CMark for MCIP2 Artix Board (Red)
The minimum CMark version necessary for AL- 84620 (Chameleon MCIP2 Artix) when used in a 7340/7440
laser is CMark 4.15.2 . If CMark 4.15.0 or 4.15.1 are used with a 7340/7440, erroneous air pressure errors and
reduced print quality after using the Focus Finder may occur, which could be mistaken for galvo issues.
76
Rev AC

Supply Unit
Main Components

F IGURE 3- 2: Main components of Chameleon MCIP board.
QSeven Module (Q7)
The QSeven module is the communication gateway for:
l
Ethernet
l
Serial port
l
SD card
l
EEPROM
l
USB
The QSeven Module acts as an interface between the Chameleon board and the Customer Interface.
Chameleon MCIP boards have a QSeven module with 2 GB of RAM and 8 GB of Flash storage.
7230/7330 and 7340/7440 lasers can only use multi- core boards. Single- core boards require CMarks which do not recognize
these laser models.
You can distinguish between single and multi- core boards by looking at the Q7 module. A Q7 with silver pins on the heat sink
denotes a Single Core Chameleon while a Q7 with a linear heat sink (fins) is present on all generations of Multi- Core Chameleon
boards. Beginning with the the Multi- Core Industrial Protocols Chameleon (MCIP) version, there is a connector for the optional
MIC board between the Q7 and the dip switches. This connector is not present on the green Multi- Core board which was the first
of the Multi- Core boards. There are several generations of Multi- Core Chameleon boards, the most recent of which do not have
78
Rev AC

Supply Unit
an SD card slot. Boards without SD card slot can still inherit customer data from SD card, but require use of PC with SD card
reader connected and minimum CMark version 4.16 installed on laser.
F IGURE 3- 3: Q7 circled. (Left) A Single Core Chameleon. (Right) A Multi- Core Chameleon.

Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA)
The FPGA interface sends received information to the QSeven and DSP .
The FPGA receives from:
l
Marking Heads (HIC)
l
Laser Source (LIC)
l
PCI Express
l
SRAM
l
CAN
l
Customer Interface
Put simply, everything feeds into the FPGA and then goes out to either the QSeven or DSP.
EEPROM
The EEPROM is the nonvolatile memory that stores the system database. The system database includes:
l
Laser type
l
Head type
l
Lens type
l
Configuration data
l
Serial number
l
IP addresses
SD Card
While there is an SD card slot present, the SD slot is not used in 7230/7330/7340/7440 laser models. CMark 4.16 and newer
offer a Customer Data feature in the Web Interface that allows inheriting of customer data from an SD card via PC when a cus -
tomer database from single core Chameleon should be inherited.
81
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
Battery
A 3V Lithium CR2032 battery located on the Chameleon board powers the memory to retain dynamic data stored in the SRAM
while the laser is powered down. This dynamic data includes:
l
Date and time (clock runs even if switched off)
l
Dynamic data for Templates/Jobs (serial numbers, variable text, shift codes, etc.)
l
Last Template/Job used
F IGURE 3- 5: Chameleon Board Battery.
Battery life is dependent on the amount of time the system is completely powered off. Typical expected battery life with a sys -
tem power on (in Standby mode) is three years. If the system is completely powered down (unplugged), expected battery life is
no more than one year.
The laser will not boot without the battery in place or if the battery is dead. Symptoms of a dead battery are the white light on
keypad on solid on start up and never beginning to flash.
Dip Switches
There is a set of four DIP switches on the control board. DIP switch 1 is usable as needed, but leave all other switches (2- 4) set to
the OFF position.
F IGURE 3- 6: Chameleon Board dip switch bank.
82
Rev AC

Supply Unit
Use DIP Switch 1 to Set the Laser IP Address
You must cycle power on the laser system after moving a DIP switch for changes to take effect.
l
ON : At boot. the TCP/IP settings from the system database are ignored and the default IP and subnet mask are used. The
settings in the system database are preserved (not erased).
The default IP address is 192.168.1.1 with subnet mask 255.255.255.0 . This is only meant for connection when primary
customer IP is unknown.
l
OFF: At boot, the TCP/IP settings configured in the system database are used.
l
The laser system will ship with DIP switch 1 set to OFF and the standard IP address pre- configured in the system database.
The other DIP switches are reserved for the internal use. Use Primary configured IP for service in the laser Web Interface.
Chameleon Board LEDs
Many diagnostic Light Emitting Diodes (LEDs ) are available on the Chameleon board for both monitoring status and diagnosing

X28 Power Supply Connector
Located on the Chameleon board, X28 is the connection for power to the Chameleon board and for the Customer Interface if
using external power and ground. The connector also contains power outputs for the marking head, supply unit cooling fan (if
present), and power to the CP- Light board and galvos.
F IGURE 3- 9: X28 connector on Chameleon board.
Connections & X28 Pin Assignment
The connections on X28 vary by laser model. The following is only valid for 7340/7440 models.

24V to fan (single, small fan in supply unit)
l

48V to CP- Light board
l
Pin 10 (GND) and 11 (V):
Power to Chameleon (48V) from electronics PSU
l
Pin 13 (V) and 14 (GND): Feedback from laser PSU (24V)

External Startup
The system can be booted by a remote source by applying a pulse from X28.5 to X28.3. Alternatively, the AutoStart Bridge (X28
pin 3- 5 bridged) will boot the system automatically after switching mains and never powers down after the shutdown sequence
(reboots automatically). This bridge can be useful in troubleshooting issues with the membrane keypad or the cable from mem -
brane keypad.
Main power must be switched OFF before rebooting or the safety system will not reset.
X19 & X20 - Supply Voltage
The Customer Interface (CI) can receive power and ground either internally or from the customer. Set the jumper configuration
on X19 and X20 to specify which method on supply will be used.
Three modes of supply are available:
l
Internal 24V CI Supply Mode: Customer interface supplied by 24V from Chameleon board. Non- isolated.
l
Internal 12V CI Supply Mode: Customer Interface supplied by 12V from Chameleon board. Non- isolated.
l
External CI Supply Mode: Customer Interface supplied by optional external customer power supply (connect incoming 12-
24V to X28.7 and incoming GND to X28.8). Isolated.
92
Rev AC

Supply Unit
Location of X19 & X20
Power supply control jumpers are located on the Chameleon board next to the connectors for power to Chameleon board (X28).
F IGURE 3- 11: Location of X19 and X20 on Chameleon board.
Jumper Positions
The positions of jumpers on pins X19 and X20 specify what method of supply for the Customer Interface will be used.

loads which are connected to the interface (24V_ CI/GND_ CI) do not exceed 250 mA in total.

HIC_ CPA
The Head Interface Card (HIC- CPA) is a communications interface between the Chameleon board and the CP- Light board.
Connectors
l
The HIC plugs directly into the Chameleon board's HIC slot.
l
Low Voltage Differential Signal (LVDS ) is generated by the Chameleon board and sent to the marking head from the HIC-
CPA board through an Ethernet cable. LVDS is the digital signal that carries galvo control commands which are then inter -
preted and converted to analog galvo commands by the CP- Light board.
l
A shutter connection on the HIC receives data from the galvos and CP- Light board to let the Chameleon know if the mirrors
are in the Shutter Open or Shutter Closed position.
l
There is a third connector (X10; white 10- pin) which is not used in the 7340/7440 laser, but which is used in other Videojet
lasers.
F IGURE 3- 13: HIC board connections identified.
100

Laser Interface Card (LIC_ JPT)
The Laser Interface Card (LIC_ JPT) connects the JPT laser source to the Chameleon board. Different Videojet lasers use different
LIC boards to accommodate the different laser sources. Both the 7340 and 7440 use the same LIC_ JPT board.
Location
F IGURE 3- 15: Photo of Chameleon board with LIC_ JPT highlighted in orange.
The LIC_ JPT board plugs directly into the Chameleon. There are two integrated ribbon cables that connect the LIC_ JPT to the
JPT laser source. The ribbon cables can be disconnected from the laser source on the rear of the laser. If the ribbon cables are
damaged, the LIC_ JPT must be replaced.
The Universal Acceleration Detection Board (UADB ), which detects the orientation of the compressor, is connected to the LIC_
JPT. This board and the cable that connects it to the LIC_ JPT board were discontinued in December 2021 and will not be present
on lasers manufactured after that date. The LIC_ JPT also has an air flow sensor to measure output from the compressor.
F IGURE 3- 16: LIC_ JPT with connectors labeled. UADB cable will not be present in lasers made after December 2021.
102

PSU Electronics (48V)
The laser system contains two power supply units: a 24V power supply for powering the laser source and cooling components
and a 48V power supply for powering most other components in the system.
The 48V power supply is connected to the Chameleon. The Chameleon then supplies varying voltages to other components in
the system including:
l
CP- Light board
l
Galvos
l
Membrane keypad
l
LEC board
l
Supply unit cooling fan (small)
The 48V power supply is always powered and supplying voltage to the Chameleon board as long as the main power switch on
the rear of the laser is in the ON position. The Chameleon then switches power to the other components listed above. The
SPM/CPD do not switch the input voltage to the 48V power supply. The relays on SPM/CPD only switch the supply voltage for the
laser source.
The laser source, compressor, and three front supply unit fans are the only components for which power is not supplied by the
48V power supply.
Location
The 48 Volt power supply is located on the underside of the mounting base beneath the Chameleon board in the supply unit. To
remove or replace the power supply, you must remove the mounting base chassis to which all components inside the supply unit
(except the laser source) are mounted.
104

A 24 Volt power supply powers the laser source in the supply unit. The SPM/CPD switches AC input voltage to this 24 Volt power

that provides cooled air to the marking head.

Safety Circuit
The laser comes standard with a Safety Power Module (SPM) circuit board that is the center of the safety system in the laser. If a
Performance Level of D (shutterlock) or E (interlock) is required, then order the laser with the optional CPD3 and FASS board. It is
possible to achieve a Performance Level of D for interlock with the standard SPM board, but only when a specific switch and are
used. Any deviation from the specified parts will result in no Performance Level rating.
SPM only (included) provides:
l
Dual channel Shutterlock circuit (no Performance Level rating)
l
Dual/redundant Emergency circuit complaint to performance level D
l
Functions as an interlock with manual reset.
l
Is Performance Level D only when using specified parts and wiring
l
Dual channel Interlock circuit with or without manual reset switch.
l
No Performance Level rating
l
Must use mechanical switches/relays instead of solid state
l
Customer must supply a reset switch
CPD3 board together with FASS board (upgrade) provides:
l
Dual channel redundant door circuit compliant to performance level D (functions as Shutterlock)
l
Door circuit extension
l
Dual/redundant Emergency circuit complaint to performance level E (functions as Interlock with manual reset)
l
Emergency circuit extension
l
Manual emergency reset
l
Customer must supply a reset switch
Both the SPM and CPD3 are designed to be used with double- pole switches as two circuits are monitored.
The boards are easily distinguished visually by their color. The SPM board is white and the CPD3 is green. You can see the SPM
board or CPD3 board and its X9 connector through the cutout in the mounting plate between the Customer Interface section of
Chameleon board and the rear panel of supply unit.
108
Rev AC

Supply Unit
Expected Behavior: Interlock
Use an interlock in Mark on the Fly applications where doors are only opened to fix issues or clear product jams.
1. Shutter closes
2. System powers off supply to laser
3. Emission LED OFF
4. System reports “Interlock Open”
5. Close door/contact
- 5 second delay between door close and laser power on again
With manual reset switch
- Requires reset signal before can continue
6. System reports "Laser Ready"
Shutterlock
Use shutterlock in applications where the door will be opened/closed frequently.
1. Shutter closes
2. Laser source remains powered
3. System reports "Shutterlock Open"
4. Emission LED is ON
5. Resets when the door is closed (no reset signal necessary)
SPM Board
The Safety Power Module (SPM) is the basic safety system board used in newer 3140/3340 lasers and all 7230/7330/7340/7440
laser models. SPM is a circuit board through which main AC power runs to both the electronics and laser power supplies. As long
as the laser is plugged in, the electronics power supply and Chameleon board are always supplied power by the SPM. The SPM
switches power for the laser power supply based on interlock circuit condition and software/logic signals from the Chameleon
board.
Performance Level Rating
The SPM has no Performance Level rating for the Shutterlock function or for Interlock. A Performance Level D can be achieved
only through use of very specific parts and cables, including a manual reset switch and specific door switch. See "SPM/SPM- 16A
Wiring" on page 165 for details. Use of any switches, relays, or cables other than those specified will result in no Performance
Level rating. If the specified switch and cable are used, Performance Level D is achieved. It is not possible to achieve a Per -
formance Level with SPM for Shutterlock no matter the components connected.
109
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
l
If Performance Level D (Shutterlock/Door Circuit) or E (Interlock/Emergency Circuit) are required, a CPD3 and additional
FASS board should be ordered.
l
The SPM cannot be connected to the FASS board.
l
The SPM (white board) used in new 3140/3340 and all 7230/7330/340/7440 lasers is a different board from the SPM-
16A (red board) and SPM- 16A- FASS (blue board) used in 3640 lasers. Take care when ordering spare parts as these boards
are not interchangeable .
Location
The SPM board is mounted on the underside of the mounting plate within the supply unit. The mains power connector assembly
is part of the SPM board and is accessible through the rear of the supply unit. The SPM power connector assembly also contains a
fuse insert with two 8 Amp 250V fuses and a main power switch for the system. The SPM uses a power cord with C13 connector.
F IGURE 3- 23: SPM Board mount location.
Installing a New SPM - Bracketry Note
If a replacement SPM must be installed, it is necessary to remove the metal bracket surrounding the power
cord receptacle from the old board and to move it to the new board before installation. Spare boards do not
include this bracket. If the bracket is missing then one of the two screw holes needed to attach the SPM to the
rear of the supply cabinet will not be present.
110
Rev AC

Supply Unit
F IGURE 3- 24: Power cord receptacle, fuse insert, and main power switch on SPM board.
Compatibility
The SPM board (white) is only used in the 7340/7440 laser systems. This circuit board cannot be used with an optional FASS
board. If a Performance Level is required, then the SPM must be exchanged for a CPD and FASS boards. The CPD plus FASS
provides a dual channel emergency circuit with manual reset switch compliant to Performance Level E or door circuit (shutterlock)
compliant to Performance Level D.
Relays
The relays K1 and K2 are closed when the two circuits of the interlock wiring are closed. K3 and K4 are then driven by the
Chameleon board, which monitors the emissions LEDs on the marking head and keypad, as well as shutter status. The
Chameleon sends signals to close the K3 and K4 relays when shutter status is known and emissions LEDs are functioning. The K5
relay is part of the circuit that detects damage to the external wiring of the interlock system.
F IGURE 3- 25: SPM Relays.
111
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide

Relay Driver/Function
K1 Interlock channel 1
K2 Interlock channel 2
K3 Chameleon board (software/logic/shutter)
K4 Chameleon board (software/logic/shutter)
K5 Part of error detection circuit for Performance Level D
Connectors
There are two ribbon cables mounted on the SPM that connect to the Chameleon board. These are the only connections used
with the SPM. There is no possibility to connect to an optional FASS board. If a FASS is required, use a CPD3 instead of an SPM.
LEDs and Fuse
F IGURE 3- 26: SPM fuse and LEDs.
Located next to the X9 connector (visible inside the supply unit between the Customer Interface section and the rear panel of the
supply unit) are five LEDs and a single fuse. The fuse is part of the error detection circuit that protects the SPM and associated
components in case of damage to external interlock wiring/cables. This fuse is replaceable with fuse specification Little Fuse 1
Amp Slo- Blo 125V max .

CPD3 + FASS
In cases where a Performance Level "d" for Shutterlock/Door Circuit or "e" for Interlock/Emergency Circuit is necessary, a com -
bined assembly that utilizes a Cobra Power Distribution (CPD3) board and FASS (FOBA ALLTEC Sicherheitssteuerung) board is
used instead of the standard SPM board assembly. The CPD3 and FASS boards are not available as a spare parts kit and must be
ordered separately, unless the upgrade is ordered as part of initial build. CPD and FASS must be used together in the 7340/7440 .
SPM can only be used alone and cannot be used in combination with FASS.
To ensure correct functionality, you must configure the boards used through TCS+ or the Laser Web Interface. See "Edit Laser
Configuration" on page 397 for TCS+ instructions.

CPD Relays
The CPD3 board has 5 relays that close at different times to allow power to the laser beam source. If all necessary relays are not
closed, then no AC voltage will be sent to the power supply for laser source. Relay state does not affect system power
(Chameleon, marking head, etc.), only laser source power.
F IGURE 3- 31: Relays on CPD3 board - numbered.

beam source (key switch closed signal to Chameleon
then to CPD)

beam source (key switch closed signal to Chameleon
then to CPD3)
3 Reacts on a safety logic (when the signal ‘safe laser Chameleon/software
power on‘   is missing, K3 switches off the laser beam
source too)
4 Not used
8 Not used

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
Laser Source (s) & Umbilical
The laser beam is generated by the laser source, which uses diodes to excite Ytterbium contained within itself. The umbilical and
collimator are part of laser source. These components are not separable and spares will ship as single, complete assembly. The col -
limator is very carefully adjusted in manufacturing and cannot be adjusted in the field.
Also part of the umbilical and laser source are the marking head cable and air hose. The marking head cable connects the HIC_
CPA and CP- Light to the galvos in the marking unit. The air hose connects the compressor and the marking unit. If any of these
parts of the umbilical or the laser source itself become damaged, then you must replace the entire source.
The laser source creates a wavelength between 1,040- 1,090 nanometers and is manufactured by JPT.
There are three laser sources available for the 7340/7440:
l
20 Watt with 3 meter umbilical (7340)
l
30 Watt with 3 meter umbilical (7440)
l
30 Watt with 10 meter umbilical (7440)
The length of the umbilical is written to an EEPROM on the CP- Light board. You cannot change to a source with a different length
umbilical without resetting this code. Only a Service level User in TCS+ can reset this code. The code which is automatically detec -
ted by the CP- Light board by looking to the laser source. This is necessary due to the difference in resistance on 3 meter versus 10
meter marking head cable.
F IGURE 3- 37: Location of laser source within the supply unit.
120
Rev AC

Supply Unit
F IGURE 3- 38: Laser source and umbilical.
121
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
Connections
The laser source is powered by 24V from the laser power supply sent via the Terminal Block. There are also two ribbon cables com -
ing from the LIC_ JPT board connected to the rear of the laser source. These connections can be seen by removing the lower plate
on the rear of the supply unit, though an air deflector makes it difficult to access the connectors.
Expected Output
Measured directly out of the collimator, the output power should be at least 20 Watts (7340) or 30 Watts (7440), but this is
before the beam goes through the marking unit lenses and mirrors. At the output of the marking head, the expected power will
be less due to the number of mirrors involved in the beam path in marking unit.
Table 3- 20 below shows the expected output as measured straight out of collimator and out of the marking head itself. Meas -
uring straight out of collimator requires some disassembly within the marking head. When measuring power on either the 7340
or 7440, always set the frequency to 30 kHz on both models for maximum average power.
Table 3- 20: Expected Output Power (Watts)
Output
Model
Directly from Collimator From Marking Head
7340 20.5 17.6
7440 30.5 26.4
Air Knife Effect - Measurement Distance
The positive air that cools the marking unit and acts as an air knife at the marking unit output can contribute
to erroneous readings by cooling the power meter sensor. The recommended distance from power meter
sensor is as follows:
Marking Head Distance from Power Meter Sensor
CFS/CFT - Small 350mm
CFS/CFT - Medium 400mm
CFS/CFT - Large 450mm

Rev AC

Supply Unit
Miscellaneous Interface Controller
(MIC)
The Miscellaneous Interface Controller (MIC), also known as the HMS Module, is a daughter board that contains the ports, firm -
ware, and hardware for use with EtherNet/IP or PROFINET. This board is only compatible with the Chameleon MCIP or newer and
will not work with any previous versions of the Chameleon as those version have no connector for the MIC board. A minimum
CMark version of 4.5 is required to recognize the MIC, but CMark 4.9 or newer is recommended as major changes to laser
statuses were introduced in CMark 4.9.
There are two versions of the MIC board: EtherNet/IP (Kit, Rating, Modul, HMS, EtherNet IP) and PROFINET (Kit, Rating, Modul,
HMS, PROFINET). They are not interchangeable and cannot be reprogrammed in the field. A sticker on one of the RJ45 ports will
have a blue dot for EtherNet/IP or yellow dot for PROFINET.
The MIC has two RJ45 ports. These should only be used for EtherNet/IP or PROFINET connection and not for TCP/IP connection
such as SmartGraph or TCS+. Those should be connected via the Chameleon board RJ45 ports.
F IGURE 3- 39: MIC- EtherNet/IP with blue dot used to identify the board.
123
Rev AC

7340 7440 Technician Training Guide
F IGURE 3- 40: MIC- PROFINET board with yellow dot to identify the board.
The MIC board must be enabled and configured in the laser Web Interface or TCS+ (CMark 4.7.6 or newer) if installed. See
"External Interfaces" on page 406 for more information on enabling and configuring the MIC Board.
Certifications and Performance
PROFINET is certified as Conformance Class C and has a cycle rate of 250 microseconds. EtherNet/IP cycle time is not tested.
Performance Metrics such Product Count, Mark Count, Batch Count are not updated in real time, but are updated at least every
100 milliseconds.
Certifications are available on Sharepoint.
Typologies supported:
l
Star
l
Open Ring (Daisy Chain)
l
Closed Ring
Limitations
l
CLARiTY Laser Controller cannot be used with EtherNet/IP or PROFINET. SmartGraph, TCS+, and most SARs (including
Ultimate) can be used with EtherNet/IP and PROFINET.
l
CPU load is increased by approximately 10% when EtherNet/IP or PROFINET is used.
l
Only ASCII characters are supported. No Unicode may be used.
l
Maximum of five variable fields per transmission

Files Required for Customer PLC
EtherNet/IP requires the customer to download an EDS file for their PLC setup. There are several versions of the EDS file for down -
load based on the model of laser. Download these from the ODVA marketplace website, from the VTI Support & Technology
team space, or locate the file and user guide on the CD included with the laser or the MIC spare part.
PROFINET requires the customer to download a GSDML file. There is only one version of this file for Videojet lasers. Download
this file from the ProfiBUS website, from the VTI Support & Technology team space, or locate it on the CD included with the laser
or with the MIC spare part.

Terminal Block
The terminal block (X100) in the 7340/7440 laser system distributes 24V from the laser power supply to internal components
related to the beam source and 48V from electronics power supply for the optional WiFi kit.

Connections
Each terminal block has one input and two outputs. Block 7 and block 8 outputs are only used if the optional W- Fi kit is installed.
The 24V laser power supply only supplies power when the key switch is in the armed position and there are no faults. Therefore,
the beam source, compressor, and cooling fans on the front of unit are all powered on after the key switch is turned. There are
two sets of wires for the beam source power, one large set and one small set. One set is power and ground to the source and the
other is the Housekeeping power and ground.
The 48V for the WiFi kit is always on as long as laser system supplied with AC power and the main power switch on the rear of
supply unit is ON.
